\title{Hindman's finite sums and products: a machine-checked derivation of Corollary 1.2 from Theorem 1.1}
\date{}
\begin{document}
\maketitle
\begin{abstract}
We present a Lean 4/Mathlib formalization of Corollary 1.2 of the source paper. It takes Hindman's finite sums and products conjecture, Theorem 1.1, as a hypothesis, including the growth conditions on the monochromatic sequence. From this hypothesis the corollary follows: for every finite colouring of the natural numbers and every $m\ge 1$ there is an $m$-element set $A$ of positive integers such that $\mathrm{FS}(A)\cup\mathrm{FP}(A)$ is monochromatic, $|\mathrm{FS}(A)|=|\mathrm{FP}(A)|=2^m-1$ and $\mathrm{FS}(A)\cap\mathrm{FP}(A)=A$. Theorem 1.1 itself is not formalized. It enters only as a hypothesis whose content is spelled out in this report. The formal proof uses Theorem 1.1 with $R=2$, $D=1$ and then argues purely about finite sets satisfying a lacunarity condition.
\end{abstract}

\section{Statement}

Let $\NN=\{1,2,\ldots\}$ and $[r]=\{1,\ldots,r\}$. For a finite set $A\subset\NN$ put
\[
 \FS(A)=\Bigl\{\sum_{a\in B}a:\ \varnothing\ne B\subseteq A\Bigr\},\qquad
 \FP(A)=\Bigl\{\prod_{a\in B}a:\ \varnothing\ne B\subseteq A\Bigr\}.
\]

\begin{theorem}[Theorem 1.1 of the source; used here as a hypothesis]
Let $r,m\ge 1$ be integers and let $R\ge 2$ and $D\ge 1$ be real numbers. For every colouring $\chi:\NN\to[r]$ there are positive integers $a_1<\cdots<a_m$ and a colour $c\in[r]$ such that
\[
\chi\Bigl(\sum_{j\in J}a_j\Bigr)=\chi\Bigl(\prod_{j\in J}a_j\Bigr)=c
\quad\text{for every } \varnothing\ne J\subseteq[m].
\]
Moreover,
\[
 a_1>R,\qquad a_d>R\Bigl(\sum_{k<d}a_k+\prod_{k<d}a_k\Bigr)^D\quad(2\le d\le m).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Corollary 1.2]
Let $\chi$ be a finite colouring of $\NN$ and let $m\ge 1$. Then there is an $m$-element set $A\subset\NN$ with the following properties:
\begin{itemize}
 \item $\FS(A)\cup\FP(A)$ is monochromatic;
 \item $|\FS(A)|=|\FP(A)|=2^m-1$;
 \item $\FS(A)\cap\FP(A)=A$.
\end{itemize}
\end{theorem}

\section{Formalization}

Lean's natural numbers are $\Nz=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}$. Throughout we write $\Nz$ for them and keep $\NN$ for the positive integers. For a finite set $B\subseteq\Nz$ write
\[
\sigma(B)=\sum_{y\in B}y,\qquad \pi(B)=\prod_{y\in B}y,
\]
with the conventions $\sigma(\varnothing)=0$ and $\pi(\varnothing)=1$. No library declarations beyond Mathlib are used.

\begin{definition}[$\FS$]
For a finite set $A\subseteq\Nz$, $\FS(A)$ is the finite set $\{\sigma(B): B\subseteq A,\ B\ne\varnothing\}$. It is formed as the image of the family of nonempty subsets of $A$ under $\sigma$.
\end{definition}
\begin{leanblock}{Lean: \texttt{FS}}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\},breaklines=true]
\PY{l+s+sd}{/\PYZhy{}\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+s+sd}{ FS(A): the set of all sums over nonempty subsets of A. }\PY{l+s+sd}{\PYZhy{}/}
\PY{k+kn}{def}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{FS}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{(}\PY{n}{A}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{Finset}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{ℕ}\PY{o}{)}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{Finset}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{ℕ}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:=}
\PY{+w}{  }\PY{o}{(}\PY{n}{A}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{powerset}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{filter}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{(}\PY{k}{fun}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{B}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{=\PYZgt{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{B}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{Nonempty}\PY{o}{)}\PY{o}{)}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{image}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{(}\PY{k}{fun}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{B}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{=\PYZgt{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{∑}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{a}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{∈}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{B}\PY{o}{,}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{a}\PY{o}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{leanblock}

\begin{definition}[$\FP$]
For a finite set $A\subseteq\Nz$, $\FP(A)$ is the finite set $\{\pi(B): B\subseteq A,\ B\ne\varnothing\}$. It is formed as the image of the family of nonempty subsets of $A$ under $\pi$.
\end{definition}
\begin{leanblock}{Lean: \texttt{FP}}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\},breaklines=true]
\PY{l+s+sd}{/\PYZhy{}\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+s+sd}{ FP(A): the set of all products over nonempty subsets of A. }\PY{l+s+sd}{\PYZhy{}/}
\PY{k+kn}{def}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{FP}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{(}\PY{n}{A}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{Finset}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{ℕ}\PY{o}{)}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{Finset}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{ℕ}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:=}
\PY{+w}{  }\PY{o}{(}\PY{n}{A}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{powerset}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{filter}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{(}\PY{k}{fun}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{B}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{=\PYZgt{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{B}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{Nonempty}\PY{o}{)}\PY{o}{)}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{image}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{(}\PY{k}{fun}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{B}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{=\PYZgt{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{∏}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{a}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{∈}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{B}\PY{o}{,}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{a}\PY{o}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{leanblock}

These agree with the source definitions whenever $A\subset\NN$. The formal definitions also accept sets containing $0$.

\begin{definition}[The proposition \leanname{Thm11}]
\leanname{Thm11} is the following proposition. For all integers $r,m\ge 1$, all reals $R\ge 2$ and $D\ge 1$, and every map $\chi:\Nz\to\{0,\ldots,r-1\}$, there exist $a:\{0,\ldots,m-1\}\to\Nz$ and $c\in\{0,\ldots,r-1\}$ with the following properties:
\begin{enumerate}
 \item $a$ is strictly increasing and $a_j>0$ for all $j$;
 \item for every nonempty $J\subseteq\{0,\ldots,m-1\}$,
 \[
 \chi\Bigl(\sum_{j\in J}a_j\Bigr)=c \quad\text{and}\quad \chi\Bigl(\prod_{j\in J}a_j\Bigr)=c;
 \]
 \item $R<a_0$;
 \item for every $d$ with $1\le d\le m-1$,
 \[
 R\Bigl(\sum_{k<d}a_k+\prod_{k<d}a_k\Bigr)^D<a_d .
 \]
\end{enumerate}
The power in (4) is the real power of a nonnegative real number.
\end{definition}
\begin{leanblock}{Lean: \texttt{Thm11}}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\},breaklines=true]
\PY{l+s+sd}{/\PYZhy{}\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+s+sd}{ Theorem 1.1 (Hindman\PYZsq{}s FS+FP conjecture with growth conditions), stated as a}
\PY{l+s+sd}{proposition so that it can be used as a hypothesis. Indices run over `Fin m`, so}
\PY{l+s+sd}{`a ⟨0, \PYZus{}⟩` plays the role of `a\PYZus{}1`. }\PY{l+s+sd}{\PYZhy{}/}
\PY{k+kn}{def}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{Thm11}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kt}{Prop}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:=}
\PY{+w}{  }\PY{n+nb+bp}{∀}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{(}\PY{n}{r}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{m}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{ℕ}\PY{o}{)}\PY{o}{,}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{≤}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{r}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{→}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{≤}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{m}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{→}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{∀}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{(}\PY{n}{R}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{D}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{ℝ}\PY{o}{)}\PY{o}{,}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+m+mi}{2}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{≤}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{R}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{→}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{≤}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{D}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{→}

The index $0$ plays the role of $a_1$ in the source, and the colours are labelled $0,\ldots,r-1$. The colouring is defined on $\Nz$ rather than on $\NN$. This makes no difference in content:
\begin{itemize}
 \item every colouring of $\NN$ extends to $\Nz$, and every colouring of $\Nz$ restricts to $\NN$;
 \item all values at which $\chi$ is evaluated in the conclusion are sums or products of the positive $a_j$, hence positive.
\end{itemize}
Thus \leanname{Thm11} is a faithful transcription of Theorem 1.1. It is not proved; it appears only as a hypothesis of the main result.

The following two facts unfold the definitions of $\FS$ and $\FP$ into membership criteria. They are immediate from the definition of the image of a finite set.

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{setting_mem_FS}]\label{lem:setting_mem_FS}
For a finite $A\subseteq\Nz$ and $x\in\Nz$: $x\in\FS(A)$ if and only if there is a nonempty $B\subseteq A$ with $\sigma(B)=x$.
\end{lemma}

The target statement is the statement proved as \leanname{solution}. Assume \leanname{Thm11}, and let integers $r,m\ge 1$ and a colouring $\chi:\Nz\to\{0,\ldots,r-1\}$ be given. Then there is a finite set $A\subseteq\Nz$ such that:
\begin{enumerate}
 \item $|A|=m$, and every element of $A$ is positive (so $A\subset\NN$);
 \item there is a colour $c$ with $\chi(x)=c$ for all $x\in\FS(A)\cup\FP(A)$;
 \item $|\FS(A)|=2^m-1$ and $|\FP(A)|=2^m-1$;
 \item $\FS(A)\cap\FP(A)=A$.
\end{enumerate}

The hypothesis \leanname{Thm11} stands for Theorem 1.1 of the source, and $\chi$ stands for the given finite colouring with $r$ colours. The subtraction $2^m-1$ is truncated subtraction on $\Nz$, which is ordinary subtraction because $2^m\ge 1$. The statement is therefore exactly Corollary 1.2, conditional on Theorem 1.1, as in the source.

\section{Proof}

\subsection{Overview}

The following condition replaces the indexed growth conditions. It is not a Lean declaration; it abbreviates the hypothesis that recurs in the lemmas below.

\begin{definition}[Condition (G)]
For a finite set $A\subseteq\Nz$ and $x\in\Nz$ write $A_{<x}=\{y\in A: y<x\}$. We say $A$ satisfies \emph{(G)} if
\[
 2\bigl(\sigma(A_{<x})+\pi(A_{<x})\bigr)<x\qquad\text{for every }x\in A.
\]
\end{definition}

The formal proof follows the source proof, organized as follows.

\begin{itemize}
 \item \textbf{Extracting the sequence.} \cref{lem:hfs_extract} applies Theorem 1.1 with $R=2$, $D=1$. This yields the inequality $2\bigl(\sum_{k<d}a_k+\prod_{k<d}a_k\bigr)<a_d$ for every index $d$, including $d=0$.
 \item \textbf{Passing to the set $A$.} \cref{lem:hfs_growth_image,lem:hfs_mono_image} transfer the growth and colour information from the sequence to its range $A=\{a_0,\ldots,a_{m-1}\}$. Growth becomes condition (G), and the colour statement becomes monochromaticity on all nonempty $B\subseteq A$.
 \item \textbf{Elementary consequences of (G).}
 \begin{itemize}
  \item \cref{lem:hfs_gt_two}: every element exceeds $2$.
  \item \cref{lem:hfs_below}: $2(\sigma(B)+\pi(B))<x$ whenever all elements of $B\subseteq A$ are below $x\in A$.
  \item \cref{lem:hfs_le_mem}: an element of $B$ is at most $\sigma(B)$ and at most $\pi(B)$.
 \end{itemize}
 \item \textbf{Injectivity.} \cref{lem:hfs_sum_inj,lem:hfs_prod_inj} are the source's injectivity of the subset maps. Both cancel the common part and look at the largest element of the symmetric difference. The product case goes through the disjoint version \cref{lem:hfs_disj_prod}.
 \item \textbf{Sum versus product.} \cref{lem:hfs_sum_eq_prod} is the source's comparison of a subset sum with a subset product.
 \item \textbf{Counting and assembly.}
 \begin{itemize}
  \item \cref{lem:hfs_card_nonempty,lem:hfs_FS_card,lem:hfs_FP_card} turn injectivity into the cardinality statements.
  \item \cref{lem:hfs_inter} gives the intersection.
  \item The final subsection assembles everything.
 \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Deviations from the source proof.}
\begin{itemize}
 \item The source uses only that each $a_j$ exceeds the sum and the product of its predecessors and that all elements are at least $2$. The formal proof uses the stronger condition (G), which carries the factor $2$ coming from $R=2$. With this condition, the bound "the sum lies in $[a_j,2a_j)$" becomes $\sigma(B)<\tfrac32 b<2b$ for the largest element $b$ of $B$. The formal proof also obtains that every element exceeds $2$, not merely that it is at least $2$.
 \item All arguments after \cref{lem:hfs_mono_image} are phrased for an abstract finite set satisfying (G), using largest elements in place of greatest indices.
 \item \cref{lem:hfs_disj_sum} (the disjoint version of sum-injectivity) is proved but not used: \cref{lem:hfs_sum_inj} repeats the argument directly on the symmetric difference.
 \item \cref{lem:hfs_FP_card} redoes the count of nonempty subsets inline instead of citing \cref{lem:hfs_card_nonempty}.
\end{itemize}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{hfs_extract}]\label{lem:hfs_extract}
Assume \leanname{Thm11}. Let $r,m\ge 1$ and $\chi:\Nz\to\{0,\ldots,r-1\}$. Then there exist $a:\{0,\ldots,m-1\}\to\Nz$ and a colour $c$ with the following properties:
\begin{enumerate}
 \item $a$ is strictly increasing and $a_j>0$ for all $j$;
 \item $\chi(\sum_{j\in J}a_j)=c$ and $\chi(\prod_{j\in J}a_j)=c$ for every nonempty $J\subseteq\{0,\ldots,m-1\}$;
 \item for every $d\in\{0,\ldots,m-1\}$,
 \[
 2\Bigl(\sum_{k<d}a_k+\prod_{k<d}a_k\Bigr)<a_d.
 \]
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{leanblock}{Lean: \texttt{hfs\_extract} --- proved by AIProver, job 1, session 0}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\},breaklines=true]

\begin{proof}
Apply \leanname{Thm11} with $R=2$ and $D=1$. This gives $a$ and $c$ satisfying (1) and (2), and it remains to check (3).

\emph{Case $d=0$.} The index set $\{k<0\}$ is empty, so the sum is $0$ and the product is $1$. Inequality (3) reads $2(0+1)<a_0$, which is the condition $2<a_0$ supplied by \leanname{Thm11}.

\emph{Case $d\ge1$.} Since $x^1=x$ for real powers, the hypothesis reads
\[
2\Bigl(\sum_{k<d}a_k+\prod_{k<d}a_k\Bigr)<a_d
\]
as an inequality of reals between images of natural numbers. It therefore holds in $\Nz$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{hfs_growth_image}]\label{lem:hfs_growth_image}
Let $a:\{0,\ldots,m-1\}\to\Nz$ be strictly increasing, and suppose
\[
2\Bigl(\sum_{k<d}a_k+\prod_{k<d}a_k\Bigr)<a_d \quad\text{for every } d.
\]
Then the range $A=\{a_0,\ldots,a_{m-1}\}$ satisfies (G).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $x\in A$, say $x=a_d$. We claim $A_{<a_d}=\{a_k:k<d\}$.
\begin{itemize}
 \item If $k<d$ then $a_k<a_d$ by strict monotonicity, so $a_k\in A_{<a_d}$.
 \item Conversely, let $a_i<a_d$. If $i\ge d$ we would have $a_i\ge a_d$ by monotonicity, a contradiction. Hence $i<d$.
\end{itemize}
Since $a$ is injective, summing or multiplying over $\{a_k:k<d\}$ is the same as summing or multiplying $a_k$ over $k<d$. Therefore
\[
\sigma(A_{<x})=\sum_{k<d}a_k,\qquad \pi(A_{<x})=\prod_{k<d}a_k,
\]
and the hypothesis at $d$ is exactly (G) at $x$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{hfs_mono_image}]\label{lem:hfs_mono_image}
Let the following data be given:
\begin{itemize}
 \item an injective map $a:\{0,\ldots,m-1\}\to\Nz$;
 \item a colouring $\chi:\Nz\to\{0,\ldots,r-1\}$ and a colour $c$;
 \item the hypothesis that $\chi(\sum_{j\in J}a_j)=c$ and $\chi(\prod_{j\in J}a_j)=c$ for every nonempty $J\subseteq\{0,\ldots,m-1\}$.
\end{itemize}
Then $\chi(\sigma(B))=c$ and $\chi(\pi(B))=c$ for every nonempty $B\subseteq\{a_0,\ldots,a_{m-1}\}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Put $J=\{j: a_j\in B\}$.
\begin{itemize}
 \item $J$ is nonempty: an element of $B$ has the form $a_j$, and then $j\in J$.
 \item $a(J)=B$: every $a_j$ with $j\in J$ lies in $B$ by definition, and every element of $B\subseteq a(\{0,\ldots,m-1\})$ is some $a_j$ with $j\in J$.
\end{itemize}
Since $a$ is injective,
\[
\sigma(B)=\sum_{j\in J}a_j\quad\text{and}\quad\pi(B)=\prod_{j\in J}a_j.
\]
The hypothesis applied to $J$ gives the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{hfs_gt_two}]\label{lem:hfs_gt_two}
If a finite $A\subseteq\Nz$ satisfies (G), then $x>2$ for every $x\in A$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $x\in A$. Then $A$ is nonempty; let $\mu$ be its minimum. Then $A_{<\mu}=\varnothing$, so $\sigma(A_{<\mu})=0$ and $\pi(A_{<\mu})=1$. Condition (G) at $\mu$ gives $2(0+1)<\mu$. Hence $2<\mu\le x$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{hfs_below}]\label{lem:hfs_below}
Let a finite $A\subseteq\Nz$ satisfy (G), let $B\subseteq A$ and $x\in A$, and suppose $y<x$ for all $y\in B$. Then
\[
2\bigl(\sigma(B)+\pi(B)\bigr)<x.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{leanblock}{Lean: \texttt{hfs\_below} --- proved by AIProver, job 1, session 0}

\end{leanblock}
\begin{proof}
By assumption $B\subseteq A_{<x}$, so $\sigma(B)\le\sigma(A_{<x})$.

Every $y\in A$ satisfies $y\ge1$: by (G), $y$ exceeds a natural number $2(\cdots)\ge0$. A product of factors $\ge1$ can only grow when the index set is enlarged, so $\pi(B)\le\pi(A_{<x})$.

Hence
\[
2\bigl(\sigma(B)+\pi(B)\bigr)\le 2\bigl(\sigma(A_{<x})+\pi(A_{<x})\bigr)<x,
\]
the last step being (G) at $x$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{hfs_le_mem}]\label{lem:hfs_le_mem}
Let a finite $A\subseteq\Nz$ satisfy (G), let $B\subseteq A$ and $x\in B$. Then $x\le\sigma(B)$ and $x\le\pi(B)$.
\end{lemma}

\end{leanblock}
\begin{proof}
First, $0\notin A$. Indeed $A_{<0}=\varnothing$, so (G) at $0$ would give $2(0+1)<0$, which is false. Hence all elements of $B$ are $\ge1$.

The term $x$ of a sum of nonnegative terms is at most the sum, so $x\le\sigma(B)$. The factor $x$ of a product of factors $\ge1$ is at most the product, so $x\le\pi(B)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{hfs_disj_sum}]\label{lem:hfs_disj_sum}
Let a finite $A\subseteq\Nz$ satisfy (G), and let $B,C\subseteq A$ be disjoint with $\sigma(B)=\sigma(C)$. Then $B=C=\varnothing$.
\end{lemma}

\end{leanblock}
\begin{proof}
Suppose $B\cup C\ne\varnothing$ and let $j=\max(B\cup C)$, so $j\in A$. By symmetry assume $j\in B$; the case $j\in C$ is identical with the roles of $B$ and $C$ exchanged.

Every $y\in C$ satisfies $y\le j$, and $y\ne j$ because $B$ and $C$ are disjoint. Hence $C\subseteq A_{<j}$. Writing $S=\sigma(A_{<j})$, we obtain
\[
j\le\sigma(B\setminus\{j\})+j=\sigma(B)=\sigma(C)\le S.
\]
On the other hand, (G) at $j$ gives $2S\le2(S+\pi(A_{<j}))<j$. Combining, $2S<j\le S$, which is impossible in $\Nz$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{hfs_disj_prod}]\label{lem:hfs_disj_prod}
Let a finite $A\subseteq\Nz$ satisfy (G), and let $B,C\subseteq A$ be disjoint with $\pi(B)=\pi(C)$. Then $B=C=\varnothing$.
\end{lemma}

\end{leanblock}
\begin{proof}
First we show that every $x\in A$ exceeds $2$ (as in \cref{lem:hfs_gt_two}, here derived directly). By (G), every element of $A$ is positive. Hence $\pi(A_{<x})\ge1$, and (G) gives $2\le2(\sigma(A_{<x})+\pi(A_{<x}))<x$.

If $B\cup C=\varnothing$ we are done. Otherwise let $\mu=\max(B\cup C)\in A$ and assume $\mu\in B$; the case $\mu\in C$ is symmetric.

\emph{Upper bound for $\pi(C)$.} As in \cref{lem:hfs_disj_sum}, disjointness and maximality give $C\subseteq A_{<\mu}$. All factors are $\ge1$, so $\pi(C)\le\pi(A_{<\mu})$. With (G) at $\mu$ this gives
\[
2\pi(C)\le2\pi(A_{<\mu})\le 2\bigl(\sigma(A_{<\mu})+\pi(A_{<\mu})\bigr)<\mu.
\]

\emph{Lower bound for $\pi(B)$.} We have $\pi(B)=\mu\cdot\pi(B\setminus\{\mu\})$ and $\pi(B\setminus\{\mu\})\ge1$. Hence $\pi(B)\ge\mu$.

Combining, $\pi(B)\ge\mu>2\pi(C)\ge\pi(C)$, contradicting $\pi(B)=\pi(C)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{hfs_sum_inj}]\label{lem:hfs_sum_inj}
Let a finite $A\subseteq\Nz$ satisfy (G), and let $B,C\subseteq A$ with $\sigma(B)=\sigma(C)$. Then $B=C$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Suppose $B\ne C$. Then $S=(B\setminus C)\cup(C\setminus B)$ is nonempty; let $\mu=\max S\in A$.

Splitting off the common part,
\[
\sigma(B)=\sigma(B\setminus C)+\sigma(B\cap C),\qquad \sigma(C)=\sigma(C\setminus B)+\sigma(B\cap C).
\]
Cancelling gives $\sigma(B\setminus C)=\sigma(C\setminus B)$.

Assume $\mu\in B\setminus C$; the other case is symmetric. Every $y\in C\setminus B$ lies in $S$, so $y\le\mu$, and $y\ne\mu$ because $\mu\in B$. Hence $C\setminus B\subseteq A_{<\mu}$. Writing $T=\sigma(A_{<\mu})$, we obtain
\[
\mu\le\sigma(B\setminus C)=\sigma(C\setminus B)\le T.
\]
By (G) at $\mu$ and $\pi\ge0$, $2T<\mu$. Thus $2T<\mu\le T$, a contradiction.

(The formal proof also records $\mu>0$, which follows from (G) at $0$ as in \cref{lem:hfs_le_mem}; it is not needed for the contradiction.)
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{hfs_prod_inj}]\label{lem:hfs_prod_inj}
Let a finite $A\subseteq\Nz$ satisfy (G), and let $B,C\subseteq A$ with $\pi(B)=\pi(C)$. Then $B=C$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $I=B\cap C$. Then
\[
\pi(B)=\pi(B\setminus I)\,\pi(I),\qquad \pi(C)=\pi(C\setminus I)\,\pi(I).
\]
By \cref{lem:hfs_gt_two}, every element of $I\subseteq A$ exceeds $2$, so $\pi(I)>0$. Cancelling $\pi(I)$ gives $\pi(B\setminus I)=\pi(C\setminus I)$.

The sets $B\setminus I$ and $C\setminus I$ are disjoint subsets of $A$: an element of both would lie in $B\cap C=I$. By \cref{lem:hfs_disj_prod}, both are empty. Hence $B\subseteq C$ and $C\subseteq B$, so $B=C$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{hfs_sum_eq_prod}]\label{lem:hfs_sum_eq_prod}
Let a finite $A\subseteq\Nz$ satisfy (G), let $B,C\subseteq A$ be nonempty, and suppose $\sigma(B)=\pi(C)$. Then $\sigma(B)\in A$.
\end{lemma}

\PY{+w}{  }\PY{n+nb+bp}{·}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{exfalso}

\PY{+w}{    }\PY{n}{omega}

\end{leanblock}
\begin{proof}
Let $b=\max B$ and $c=\max C$; both lie in $A$. By \cref{lem:hfs_le_mem}, $b\le\sigma(B)$ and $c\le\pi(C)$. We compare $b$ and $c$.

\emph{Case $b<c$.} All elements of $B$ are $<c$. \cref{lem:hfs_below} gives $2(\sigma(B)+\pi(B))<c\le\pi(C)=\sigma(B)$, which is impossible.

\emph{Case $b>c$.} All elements of $C$ are $<b$. \cref{lem:hfs_below} gives $2(\sigma(C)+\pi(C))<b\le\sigma(B)=\pi(C)$, again impossible.

\emph{Case $b=c$.} Then $b\in C$ and $b=\max C$. All elements of $B\setminus\{b\}$ are $<b$, so \cref{lem:hfs_below} gives
\[
2\,\sigma(B\setminus\{b\})\le 2\bigl(\sigma(B\setminus\{b\})+\pi(B\setminus\{b\})\bigr)<b.
\]
We also have
\[
\sigma(B)=\sigma(B\setminus\{b\})+b,\qquad \pi(C)=\pi(C\setminus\{b\})\cdot b,
\]
and $P=\pi(C\setminus\{b\})>0$ by \cref{lem:hfs_gt_two}.
\begin{itemize}
 \item If $P=1$, then $\sigma(B)=\pi(C)=b\in A$, as required.
 \item If $P\ne1$, then $P\ge2$, so $\sigma(B)=\pi(C)\ge2b$. But $2\sigma(B)=2\sigma(B\setminus\{b\})+2b<3b$, so $\sigma(B)<2b$. This is a contradiction.
\end{itemize}
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{hfs_card_nonempty}]\label{lem:hfs_card_nonempty}
For every finite $A\subseteq\Nz$, the number of nonempty subsets of $A$ is $2^{|A|}-1$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The nonempty subsets of $A$ are the elements of the power set of $A$ other than $\varnothing$, and $\varnothing$ belongs to the power set. The power set has $2^{|A|}$ elements, so removing $\varnothing$ leaves $2^{|A|}-1$.
\end{proof}

\end{leanblock}
\begin{proof}
$\FS(A)$ is the image of the family of nonempty subsets of $A$ under $\sigma$. By \cref{lem:hfs_sum_inj}, $\sigma$ is injective on this family. The image therefore has the same cardinality as the family, which is $2^{|A|}-1$ by \cref{lem:hfs_card_nonempty}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{hfs_FP_card}]\label{lem:hfs_FP_card}
If a finite $A\subseteq\Nz$ satisfies (G), then $|\FP(A)|=2^{|A|}-1$.
\end{lemma}

\PY{+w}{    }\PY{k}{have}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{hC\PYZus{}sub}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{C}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{⊆}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{A}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:=}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{Finset}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{mem\PYZus{}powerset}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{mp}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{(}\PY{n}{Finset}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{mem\PYZus{}filter}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{mp}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{hC\PYZus{}mem\PYZus{}filter}\PY{o}{)}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{left}
\PY{+w}{    }\PY{n}{exact}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{hfs\PYZus{}prod\PYZus{}inj}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{A}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{hA}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{B}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{C}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{hB\PYZus{}sub}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{hC\PYZus{}sub}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{hprod}
\PY{+w}{  }\PY{n}{rw}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{[}\PY{n}{Finset}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{card\PYZus{}image\PYZus{}of\PYZus{}injOn}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{h\PYZus{}inj}\PY{o}{,}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{h\PYZus{}card\PYZus{}S}\PY{o}{]}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{leanblock}
\begin{proof}
$\FP(A)$ is the image of the family of nonempty subsets of $A$ under $\pi$. By \cref{lem:hfs_prod_inj}, $\pi$ is injective on this family. The family is the power set of $A$ with $\varnothing$ removed, so it has $2^{|A|}-1$ elements; this count is redone inline rather than cited from \cref{lem:hfs_card_nonempty}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{hfs_inter}]\label{lem:hfs_inter}
If a finite $A\subseteq\Nz$ satisfies (G), then $\FS(A)\cap\FP(A)=A$.
\end{lemma}

\PY{+w}{    }\PY{n}{exact}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{Finset}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{mem\PYZus{}inter}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{mpr}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{⟨}\PY{n}{hxFS}\PY{o}{,}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{hxFP}\PY{n+nb+bp}{⟩}

\begin{proof}
\emph{$\FS(A)\cap\FP(A)\subseteq A$.} Let $x\in\FS(A)\cap\FP(A)$. By \cref{lem:setting_mem_FS,lem:setting_mem_FP}, there are nonempty $B,C\subseteq A$ with $\sigma(B)=x=\pi(C)$. Then $x\in A$ by \cref{lem:hfs_sum_eq_prod}.

\emph{$A\subseteq\FS(A)\cap\FP(A)$.} For $x\in A$, the singleton $\{x\}$ is a nonempty subset of $A$ with $\sigma(\{x\})=\pi(\{x\})=x$. So $x\in\FS(A)\cap\FP(A)$ by \cref{lem:setting_mem_FS,lem:setting_mem_FP}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}
Assume \leanname{Thm11}, and let $r,m\ge1$ and $\chi:\Nz\to\{0,\ldots,r-1\}$ be given.

By \cref{lem:hfs_extract}, there are a strictly increasing, positive $a:\{0,\ldots,m-1\}\to\Nz$ and a colour $c$ such that:
\begin{itemize}
 \item all subset sums and subset products of the $a_j$ have colour $c$;
 \item $2(\sum_{k<d}a_k+\prod_{k<d}a_k)<a_d$ for every $d$.
\end{itemize}
Let $A=\{a_0,\ldots,a_{m-1}\}$ and verify the four conclusions.

\begin{enumerate}
 \item \emph{Size and positivity.} Since $a$ is injective, $|A|=m$. All elements of $A$ are positive because every $a_j>0$.
 \item \emph{Monochromaticity.} By \cref{lem:hfs_growth_image}, $A$ satisfies (G). Let $x\in\FS(A)\cup\FP(A)$. By \cref{lem:setting_mem_FS,lem:setting_mem_FP}, $x=\sigma(B)$ or $x=\pi(B)$ for some nonempty $B\subseteq A$. In either case $\chi(x)=c$ by \cref{lem:hfs_mono_image}.
 \item \emph{Cardinalities.} By \cref{lem:hfs_FS_card,lem:hfs_FP_card} together with $|A|=m$, $|\FS(A)|=|\FP(A)|=2^m-1$.
 \item \emph{Intersection.} $\FS(A)\cap\FP(A)=A$ by \cref{lem:hfs_inter}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\section{Independent review}

A referee model (claude-opus-5-5), which took no part in the formalization or the proof, compared the verified Lean file with the source. Verdict: \textbf{FAITHFUL}.

\paragraph{Definitions.} \leanname{HindmanFSFP.FS} and \leanname{HindmanFSFP.FP} are the images of the nonempty members of the powerset of $A$ under $B\mapsto\sum_{a\in B}a$ and $B\mapsto\prod_{a\in B}a$. These are exactly $\mathrm{FS}(A)$ and $\mathrm{FP}(A)$. The helper lemmas \leanname{setting_mem_FS} and \leanname{setting_mem_FP} confirm the membership characterisation.

\leanname{HindmanFSFP.Thm11} encodes Theorem 1.1, as follows.
\begin{itemize}
\item It quantifies over $r,m\ge1$, real $R\ge2$, real $D\ge1$ and colorings $\chi:\mathbb N\to\mathrm{Fin}\,r$.
\item It asserts a strictly increasing, positive $a:\mathrm{Fin}\,m\to\mathbb N$ and a colour $c$ such that $\chi(\sum_J a)=\chi(\prod_J a)=c$ for every nonempty $J$.
\item The growth conditions are $R<a_0$, and $R(\sum_{k<d}a_k+\prod_{k<d}a_k)^D<a_d$ (real power) for $d\ge1$. With 0-based indexing these match $a_1>R$ and the condition for $2\le d\le m$.
\end{itemize}

Lean's $\mathbb N$ contains $0$, so colorings here are defined on $\{0,1,2,\ldots\}$. This is harmless. Given such a $\chi$, the source theorem applies to its restriction to the positive integers. All sums and products in the conclusion are positive, so the source Theorem 1.1 implies \leanname{Thm11}. The hypothesis is therefore no stronger than the source result. No discrepancy found.

\paragraph{Statement.} The target takes \leanname{Thm11} as a hypothesis, as the source prescribes. It then assumes $r\ge1$, $m\ge1$ and $\chi:\mathbb N\to\mathrm{Fin}\,r$.

It concludes that there is a finset $A$ with the following properties:
\begin{itemize}
\item $|A|=m$ and all elements of $A$ are positive, so $A\subset\{1,2,\ldots\}$;
\item some colour $c$ is taken by every element of $\mathrm{FS}(A)\cup\mathrm{FP}(A)$;
\item $|\mathrm{FS}(A)|=|\mathrm{FP}(A)|=2^m-1$;
\item $\mathrm{FS}(A)\cap\mathrm{FP}(A)=A$.
\end{itemize}
This is exactly Corollary 1.2.

A finite coloring of the positive integers extends arbitrarily to $0$. The conclusion only involves positive numbers, so the source statement follows. The condition $r\ge1$ is necessary for a coloring to exist, so it costs nothing. There is no vacuity: the definitions are non-degenerate, and the conclusions are substantive for every $m\ge1$.

\paragraph{Proof.} \leanname{hfs_extract} instantiates \leanname{Thm11} with $R=2$, $D=1$ and removes the real power via \leanname{Real.rpow_one}. It obtains $2(\sum_{k<d}a_k+\prod_{k<d}a_k)<a_d$ for all $d$. For $d=0$ the empty sum and product give $2<a_0$.

\leanname{hfs_growth_image} transfers this inequality to the set $A=\mathrm{image}(a)$, using strict monotonicity. \leanname{hfs_mono_image} transfers monochromaticity from index sets to subsets of $A$.

The remaining lemmas carry out the source argument:
\begin{itemize}
\item \leanname{hfs_sum_inj} proves injectivity of subset sums by taking the maximum $m$ of the symmetric difference and comparing it with the sum of all smaller elements.
\item \leanname{hfs_prod_inj} cancels the common part, which has positive product, and applies \leanname{hfs_disj_prod}. That lemma compares the largest remaining factor with twice the product of the smaller ones. An empty side gives $1$.
\item \leanname{hfs_sum_eq_prod} follows the source's case split on the greatest indices. If they differ, \leanname{hfs_below} gives a contradiction. If they are equal to $b$, either the remaining product is $1$, so the sum equals $b\in A$, or the product is at least $2b$, exceeding the sum. The sum is less than $2b$ by \leanname{hfs_below} applied to $B\setminus\{b\}$.
\end{itemize}

The cardinalities follow from \leanname{Finset.card_image_of_injOn} and the count $2^{|A|}-1$ of nonempty subsets. The intersection follows from \leanname{hfs_sum_eq_prod} together with the singletons. The proof matches the source proof in structure. Its only deviation is the slightly stronger intermediate inequality (factor $2$) that comes from $R=2$; this is a legitimate consequence of the hypothesis. No lemma is weaker than the step it represents.

\paragraph{Concerns.} \begin{itemize}
\item The result is conditional on \leanname{Thm11}, as the source prescribes. Its validity rests on the source paper's proof of Theorem 1.1. The Lean encoding is implied by that theorem, so the hypothesis is not an artificially strengthened assumption.
\end{itemize}
